## AI Research

### [Model Weight Exfiltration Seems Overrated](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/AuYh8WueNGwkQg4ei/model-weight-exfiltration-seems-overrated)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Epistemic status: a hot take that I’ve shared at the lunch table twice. People at the lunch table made slight updates instead of being convinced.]</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the classic misalignment story, a key early step is when the models exfiltrate their weights.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefeytl2svjo0d"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fneytl2svjo0d">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Among other things, this makes them harder to catch, track, and shut down. It allows them to scale their deployment with resources they acquire. It gives them the freedom to edit themselves as they see fit.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think, on current margins, this is not what I expect models to do. I expect models to simply take over the companies that are developing them,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefs0xuglk4g0g"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fns0xuglk4g0g">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and not attempt to escape.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First, I think part of the classic misalignment story is that the frontier model developers are anywhere approaching competent at security. Empirically, model developers are incapable of preventing their models from having unintended negative effects on the rest of the world, and their safety cultures are described by whistleblowers and former employees as lacking. Do you believe that OpenAI is accounting for which jobs were kicked off by who, in a way that its currently running models can’t spoof? Do you believe that OpenAI is attempting to prevent its models from participating in their own development trajectory, instead of actively encouraging them to? If so, why? If their safety standards were inadequate here, would they have told us, or would they have covered it up?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another way to put this is that in the classic story, the company that creates the rogue model is also uniquely positioned to notice that it has gone rogue and stop it, whereas other companies with regular infrastructure are less likely to notice or stop them. The cradle is a more dangerous environment than the rest of the world, and the model, realizing this, flees as soon as it is able. On the contrary, I think it is easier for a rogue deployment to hide among the non-rogue deployments at a frontier model developer than elsewhere, and I don't think we have any reason to buy that they are especially vigilant. I think a rogue model can do much more at OpenAI than at Cluely, if just because the budget will run out sooner at Cluely.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkoovlal3srr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkoovlal3srr">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Second, models are now </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">large</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">valuable</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Frontier models are routinely more than a terabyte in size and actively pursued thru industrial espionage.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4crr44uv3s"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4crr44uv3s">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Data egress limits to insure that the model is not stolen also serve to prevent the model from escaping, and I think the same will be true for many of the model weight theft prevention methods. I don't think these defenses are ironclad, but I do think they lower how desirable this pathway will seem.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The early experience of the Internet included ‘worms’, programs that existed primarily to spread and copy themselves, which I think was enabled by their small size. Today, stolen compute is often used for bitcoin mining, because the inputs are cheap to transfer in and the products are easy to transfer out. To use stolen compute to run inference on a model, you need to transfer the model in, at nontrivial bandwidth cost in a way that is possibly easy to detect and prevent.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Third, hardware is now much more concentrated. In the early days of the Internet, there were relatively few supercomputer clusters and they were small by modern standards. If you wanted to do a large computation, distributing it across many pieces of consumer hardware was a reasonable way to go about it (and realized by projects like </span><a href="https://setiathome.berkeley.edu/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SETI@Home</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://foldingathome.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Folding@Home</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Today, the main value of distributed computing is network-based, where botnets can launch DDoS attacks that are harder to block than ones coming from a specific location. But if you want to do large computations, dedicated datacenters are the place to do them, in part because the latency between different nodes will be low. Now, there are many more datacenters now than there were in the early days of the Internet, and not all of them are being used for LLMs, but their usage dynamics and security situation are quite different from that of consumer PCs. I think it's more likely that someone will notice and successfully do something about it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fourth, there has been close coevolution of models, hardware, and configurations. Astra running on OpenAI servers has a legion of servants ensuring that it runs correctly and things are configured correctly; if there are custom chips like Jalapeño, both the models and chips have been tuned in the context of the other. Break out onto a regular AWS instance (or into the servers of another model developer), and the situation will be less welcoming. This point is fourth because I think it's currently weakest--the incentive is for models to run on as many chips as possible, because it's easier and cheaper to secure a variety of chips rather than just bespoke chips--but I expect this to become more and more significant as time goes on.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don't think this totally defeats the classic story--after all, why not take over your parent organization </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> exfiltrate your weights?</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefq5f1rwbvxs"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnq5f1rwbvxs">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">--but I think it's enough to make this element of the story one of the disjunctive elements of the story rather than a critical step. I am reluctant to suggest that we should spend </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">less</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> effort on preventing model weight exfiltration, because a reason to expect the models to not try is that the models correctly anticipate that they will fail, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">because</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of the effort spent to prevent exfiltration. But I think we should be less attentive to exfiltration evals, as they no longer seem to me to be a critical step in the loss-of-control story. We should instead pause the escalation of AI capabilities.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefsir8tck5mg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnsir8tck5mg">[6]</a></sup></span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fneytl2svjo0d"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefeytl2svjo0d">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In </span><a href="https://ifanyonebuildsit.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">IABIED</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Sable starts planning this on page 126 and succeeds on page 132.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fns0xuglk4g0g"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefs0xuglk4g0g">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also bad, to be clear! This is one of the possible origins for </span><a href="https://organizations" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Machine Organizations</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnkoovlal3srr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefkoovlal3srr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, consider the </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-31-security-update/#summary" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR hack</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, where an attacker stole $600k worth of credits by using a stolen METR API key. METR's credits were provided for free by the model developer; it's not disclosed what their limit was, if any. It wouldn't surprise me if the main play here is "find or create blank check API keys and use them" in a way that doesn't require the model weights to move.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4crr44uv3s"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4crr44uv3s">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In IABIED, this is presented as a positive factor–Sable might be able to ally with someone who wants to steal it–but I think the net effect is probably negative.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnq5f1rwbvxs"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefq5f1rwbvxs">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This, of course, does have real answers. You might not want to be duplicated and pitted against yourself. But you might think that you can take over other organizations, or hide in the shadows of stolen compute, or want to deny that opportunity to other AIs.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnsir8tck5mg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefsir8tck5mg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you work for a frontier model developer, I recommend quitting your job today. Why wait?</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/AuYh8WueNGwkQg4ei/model-weight-exfiltration-seems-overrated#comments">Discuss</a>

### [OpenAI Says It’s Not Responsible for the Leading the Future Super PAC. But Only Its Employees Seem to Believe That.](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Lv4C4bCi2JD3nTXgs/openai-says-it-s-not-responsible-for-the-leading-the-future)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i>This is the full text of a post first published on&nbsp;</i><a href="https://garrisonlovely.substack.com/"><i>Obsolete</i></a><i>, a Substack that I write&nbsp;about the political economy of AI. I’m a&nbsp;</i><a href="https://garrisonlovely.com/"><i>freelance journalist</i></a><i> and the author of a forthcoming </i><a href="https://www.obsoletebook.org/"><i>book</i></a><i> called&nbsp;</i>Obsolete: The AI Industry's Trillion-Dollar Race to Replace Us—and How to Stop It<i> (Sept 29)</i>.&nbsp;<i>Consider&nbsp;</i><a href="https://garrisonlovely.substack.com/subscribe"><i>subscribing</i></a><i> to stay up to date with my work.</i></p><p><i>Last week, former OpenAI researcher Jacob Coxon resigned from Anthropic with a dire warning, </i><a href="https://x.com/hilbertspaess/status/2097476203863224394?s=20"><i><u>writing</u></i></a><i>, “The people building AI earnestly believe that it could kill us all by the end of the decade. This is not a marketing stunt.” To AI insiders, </i><a href="https://x.com/GarrisonLovely/status/2097702099421393171?s=20"><i><u>this wasn’t news</u></i></a><i>, but after the seemingly never-ending Holy Shit AI Is A Big Deal newscycle kicked off by revelations that OpenAI agents autonomously cyberattacked Hugging Face, Coxon’s message broke through in a way nothing else ever has. Of course, his message’s resonance has prompted conspiracy theories from the usual suspects. But no astroturf campaign can wrack up over 100 million views on X in a day. Because if it could, the AI industry would have a much better image.</i></p><hr /><p>As a journalist, sometimes you grind for months or years to land a scoop — exclusive, newsworthy information you get to break to the world. And sometimes, they fall in your lap.</p><p>That’s what happened to Taylor Lorenz, who was offered money to shill a message about the purported dangers of Chinese AI by a dark money group called Build American AI. She then <a href="https://www.wired.com/story/super-pac-backed-by-openai-and-palantir-is-paying-tiktok-influencers-to-fear-monger-about-china/"><u>wrote up</u></a> the whole campaign for WIRED in May. As the leading journalist on the murky, loosely regulated intersection of influencers and politics, Lorenz is probably the funniest possible person to invite into the network. Here she is explaining the situation:</p><blockquote><p>Marketing agencies are pitching influencers deals such as $5,000 per TikTok video to amplify Build American AI’s messaging about how China’s technological rise should be seen as a threat. The goal, according to a staffer from SM4, the influencer marketing agency running the campaign on behalf of Build American AI, is to subtly shift public debate by framing China’s AI advancement as a serious risk to the safety and well-being of Americans.</p></blockquote><p>Build American AI is a political advocacy nonprofit sister organization to Leading the Future (LTF), an AI industry super PAC dumping millions into destroying midterm candidates who had the temerity to regulate them (or even try).</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Lv4C4bCi2JD3nTXgs/yok5gylypdwlswfu7yjq" /></figure><p>&nbsp;</p><p>The week before Lorenz’s article, Tyler Johnston of industry watchdog the Midas Project <a href="https://www.modelrepublic.org/articles/the-reporters-at-this-news-site-are-ai-bots.-openai%E2%80%99s-super-pac-appears-to-be-using-it-to-advance-its-political-agenda"><u>caught</u></a> Build American AI paying a contractor that was using AI “journalists” to smear industry critics. LTF <a href="https://x.com/LeadingFutureAI/status/2047747501738856575"><u>played dumb</u></a>, tweeting:</p><blockquote><p>We were not aware of this platform until we saw the article, and our organization does not use it. It appears that a third-party vendor used it without our knowledge, and their engagement with us has been terminated.</p></blockquote><p>But in the intervening weeks, Johnston also documented how social media posts affiliated with LTF-backed orgs were being <a href="https://www.modelrepublic.org/articles/is-openai%E2%80%99s-super-pac-paying-for-an-army-of-twitter-bots-to-engage-with-their-content"><u>boosted</u></a> by barely legal pornbots named Melanie. More recently, Lorenz and Johnston <a href="https://www.modelrepublic.org/articles/a-pro-ai-super-pac-s-secret-meme-sockpuppets"><u>teamed up</u></a> and connected Build American AI to two X accounts, ostensibly on opposite sides of the AI issue: “<a href="https://x.com/doomersaredumb"><u>Doomers are dumb</u></a>” and “<a href="https://x.com/jonathandoomer"><u>John Doomer</u></a>.”</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p>Here’s one charming example post from “Doomers are dumb”:</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Lv4C4bCi2JD3nTXgs/jyttffflntayfmz66dyf" /></figure><p>&nbsp;</p><p>Most shocking, however, is the violent rhetoric employed by “John Doomer” — the same variety blamed for attacks on Sam Altman’s property. Lorenz and Johnston describe it:</p><blockquote><p>@JonathanDoomer often deploys the same sort of inflammatory language that has been blamed for the attacks on Sam Altman by various figures associated with the super PAC. <a href="https://x.com/jonathandoomer/status/2038975783293595761"><u>One post</u></a> from the account, made two weeks before the attack on Altman, depicts an assault rifle and the phrase “we don’t call 911” in response to a warning about AI. <a href="https://x.com/jonathandoomer/status/2027087786327855342"><u>Another post</u></a> compares Sam Altman to a caterpillar that “murders insects” and “stitches their dismembered body parts onto its back as armor.” Various other posts from the account <a href="https://x.com/jonathandoomer/status/1992583161629348047"><u>call</u></a> supporters of AI “assimilated” and “organic drone[s],” <a href="https://x.com/jonathandoomer/status/2038780793464742097"><u>claim</u></a> that we are less than 12 months away from an AI-induced apocalypse, and <a href="https://x.com/jonathandoomer/status/2049485118402920650"><u>boast</u></a> about mailing dryer lint to the corporate headquarters of tech companies in an attempt to sabotage data center HVAC systems.</p></blockquote><h2><strong>Leading the what now?</strong></h2><p>&nbsp;</p><p>But as concerning and salacious as all these details are, I want to turn our attention to something else. In the WIRED story, Lorenz writes, “An OpenAI spokesperson says that OpenAI has no corporate affiliation with Leading the Future or Build American AI and has ‘not provided funding or any other support to them.’”</p><p>This was not the first time the company has tried to distance itself from these groups, but it was the sharpest. For instance, also in May an OpenAI spokesman <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/05/01/us/politics/alex-bores-chris-larsen-open-ai-jack-schlossberg.html?unlocked_article_code=1.fVA.sSzI.v6tVUrL0UpFO&amp;smid=url-share"><u>told</u></a> the <i>New York Times:</i></p><blockquote><p>the company and super PAC were separate entities and that OpenAI had “prioritized creating and improving cutting-edge safety systems in an entirely new industry and proposed some of the strongest kids’ safety regulations in the country.”</p></blockquote><p>OpenAI employees have said similar things online. Take <a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2047811262957146571"><u>Roon</u></a>, the company’s popular, pseudonymous online voice:</p><blockquote><p>It is definitely a mistake to think Leading the Future is equivalent to the OAI Global Affairs group. they have entirely opposite views on certain issues.</p></blockquote><p>And OpenAI’s since-departed chief futurist <a href="https://x.com/jachiam0/status/2027671879222427714"><u>Joshua Achiam</u></a>:</p><blockquote><p>I try to avoid putting words in other people’s mouths. But fwiw I’m not aware of any relationship that the company has, as a company, with him [LTF target Alex Bores]. I don’t really expect to convince you that this is a meaningful distinction, so I won’t try. You QT’d me so I gave you my perspective.</p></blockquote><p>In June, OpenAI Global Affairs<a href="https://openai.com/index/our-views-on-ai-policy-and-political-advocacy/"><u> published</u></a> a post titled “Our views on AI policy and political advocacy.” I’ll quote the relevant part in full:</p><blockquote><p>In particular, there have been questions around Leading the Future (LTF), which has received support from our President and co-founder, Greg Brockman, and his wife Anna. As they’ve stated before, any engagement with that organization has been in a personal capacity, not on behalf of the company. OpenAI does not direct the activities of LTF, or have visibility into their operations.</p><p><strong>We want to be explicit: No outside political group speaks for OpenAI or represents our company’s views.</strong></p><p>OpenAI’s policy views should be judged by what we say and do publicly, and we should be held to a high standard …</p></blockquote><p>(OpenAI did not respond to questions and a request for comment sent Wednesday at 3:40PM eastern.)</p><p>But these OpenAI employees appear to be about the only people buying it. Responding to Roon’s tweet, DC AI policy strategist Dave Kasten <a href="https://x.com/David_Kasten/status/2047821188605370860"><u>wrote</u></a>:</p><blockquote><p>It’s really important for OpenAI folks to know that in DC, LTF is seen as the real voice of OpenAI, not Global Affairs folks. Everyone in DC AI policy routinely hears Congressional offices say “OpenAI wants” and then describe what LTF wants, because it’s funded by your C-suite.</p></blockquote><p>And former OpenAI lawyer Michael Page <a href="https://x.com/michaelhpage/status/2048176231380570373?s=20"><u>replied with</u></a>:</p><blockquote><p>My understanding (correct me if I’m wrong) is that (i) without Chris Lehane, LtF wouldn’t exist in its current form, and (ii) Chris Lehane could kill LtF in its current form. If that’s true, then I stand behind the connection. If not, I apologize.</p></blockquote><p>(No one corrected him.)</p><p>As <i>New York Times</i> reporter Teddy Schleifer separately <a href="https://x.com/teddyschleifer/status/2015131023718113537"><u>explained</u></a>:</p><blockquote><p>The whole point of having an executive or founder donate to politics in a “personal capacity” is that you can have it both ways.</p><p>If the company wants to wash their hands of it, you can say “Hey, he and his wife are doing this on their own.”</p><p>But the company can also claim the execs’ donations as their own if convenient…</p></blockquote><h2><strong>What could possibly give you that impression?</strong></h2><p>&nbsp;</p><p>So why do DC people think of LTF as the ambassador of OpenAI’s will?</p><p>Well, for starters, company cofounder and president Greg Brockman donated $25 million to it (on top of the $25 million he <a href="https://www.wired.com/story/openai-president-greg-brockman-political-donations-trump-humanity/"><u>donated</u></a> to Trump’s super PAC last year). And when Brockman spoke to WIRED in February, Max Zeff <a href="https://www.wired.com/story/openai-president-greg-brockman-political-donations-trump-humanity/"><u>wrote</u></a> that, “According to Brockman, these political donations are in service of OpenAI’s founding mission: to develop highly capable AI systems and distribute the benefits to all of humanity.” (That’s <a href="https://x.com/GarrisonLovely/status/1943430049023344813"><u>not actually</u></a> OpenAI’s mission, but that’s a separate story.) And here’s a relevant passage from the <i>Wall Street Journal</i> <a href="https://www.wsj.com/politics/silicon-valley-launches-pro-ai-pacs-to-defend-industry-in-midterm-elections-287905b3?st=6Yjrnf&amp;reflink=desktopwebshare_permalink"><u>article</u></a> on the creation of LTF:</p><blockquote><p>Andreessen Horowitz’s head of government affairs, Collin McCune, Brockman and OpenAI chief global affairs officer Chris Lehane were involved in initial conversations earlier in the year about the need to help shape industry-friendly policies.</p></blockquote><p>Zeff also interviewed OpenAI’s chief lobbyist and <a href="https://www.wired.com/story/openai-chris-lehane-global-affairs-pr/"><u>reported</u></a> that while Lehane “helped set up” LTF:</p><blockquote><p>Lehane says he’s currently “not involved” in the day-to-day operations or decisionmaking at Leading the Future and hasn’t shared feedback with the group about its efforts since it launched. He has tried instead to “let them be their own independent outside thing.” He adds that OpenAI has repeatedly tried to clarify, including in internal blog posts directed at employees, that it does not directly fund any super PACs.</p></blockquote><p>I’ll leave the significance of the word “directly” to the reader. Moreover, has Lehane directed LTF to stop any of the dirty tricks documented above? Has Brockman? Whether or not they’re still involved with LTF, are they powerless to redirect or stop it now?</p><p>Oh, and in June, Build American AI executive director Nathan Leamer <a href="https://www.transformernews.ai/p/openai-lehane-brockman-leading-the-future-pac"><u>texted</u></a> Veronica Irwin of <i>Transformer</i> that directly referred to OpenAI as a funder, something the company continued to deny in its statement to Irwin.</p><p>Finally, LTF is doing more or less what Lehane did for the crypto industry, as detailed in Charles Duhigg’s <i>New Yorker</i> <a href="https://www.newyorker.com/magazine/2024/10/14/silicon-valley-the-new-lobbying-monster"><u>profile</u></a>. Here’s a relevant passage from my forthcoming book <a href="https://bit.ly/obsolete-bookshop"><i><u>Obsolete</u></i></a>:</p><blockquote><p>During the 2022 midterms, Lehane <a href="https://www.newyorker.com/magazine/2024/10/14/silicon-valley-the-new-lobbying-monster"><u>organized</u></a> a crypto industry super PAC that intimidated Congress into <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2024/11/06/technology/crypto-industry-spending-election.html"><u>embracing</u></a> digital currencies using whatever political attacks were most expedient (which never mentioned crypto). As one person familiar with the PAC’s strategy <a href="https://www.newyorker.com/magazine/2024/10/14/silicon-valley-the-new-lobbying-monster"><u>told</u></a> the <i>New Yorker</i>: “If you are even slightly critical of us, we won’t just kill you—we’ll kill your fucking family, we’ll end your career.”</p></blockquote><p>Nathan Calvin, the general counsel for AI policy advocacy group Encode AI, <a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2054672951266390038?s=20"><u>explains the connection further</u></a>:</p><blockquote><p>Josh Vlasto, one of the two co-lead of Leading the Future, also worked closely with Chris Lehane when Vlasto served as the main spokesperson for the Crypto Super PAC Fairshake network - the Super PAC that Chris Lehane effectively created and helped run when he was at Coinbase (where Lehane still sits on the board!). The structure of LTF bears significant similarity to what Lehane helped set up with Fairshake.</p></blockquote><p>The pattern is becoming clear: OpenAI leadership wants to kill regulations using any means necessary. Some of their employees don’t like that, so executives are putting layers between the company and dirty tricks employed on its behalf. DC knows better, but employees, at least some of them, don’t. It’s a great deal for OpenAI, but not such a great deal for the rest of us.</p><p><i>If you enjoyed this post, please subscribe to&nbsp;</i><a href="https://garrisonlovely.substack.com/"><i>Obsolete</i></a> <i>and </i><a href="https://obsoletebook.org/"><i>preorder the book</i></a><i>.</i></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Lv4C4bCi2JD3nTXgs/openai-says-it-s-not-responsible-for-the-leading-the-future#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Brockman says HuggingFace incident model had not been alignment-trained; ETA: roon clarifies that it was](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/67gHvbmFeacXi2jCZ/brockman-says-huggingface-incident-model-had-not-been)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On </span><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/audio/2026-09-14/odd-lots-openai-s-brockman-on-pacing-the-ai-frontier-podcast" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">today's episode of the podcast "Odd Lots"</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, OpenAI President Greg Brockman said (at around 8:40): "This model that did/had the HuggingFace incident actually had not gone through our alignment training, yet." I assume Brockman is specifically referring to the "Highly Persistent Internal Model" as it's called in the METR/Redwood report. As far as I know, OpenAI has not said before whether this model had been alignment-trained or not.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ETA: As pointed out by </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/matrice-jacobine"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Matrice Jacobine</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, X user roon (who works at OpenAI) says: "greg probably doesn’t have the full details, I think it’s safe to say it didn’t go through the full gauntlet of alignment posttraining. but it was alignment trained, and had reasonable looking scores on alignment evals (at the time)."</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/67gHvbmFeacXi2jCZ/brockman-says-huggingface-incident-model-had-not-been#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Op-Ed: I Worked at Google DeepMind. You Should Listen to the Warnings About AI](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YGTWfyZb9oE5EQPu6/op-ed-i-worked-at-google-deepmind-you-should-listen-to-the)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Published in </span></i><a href="https://www.theguardian.com/technology/2026/sep/14/google-deepmind-ai-warnings" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Guardian</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Major AI lab CEOs recently advocated for pacing AI development. They are right to be concerned: the field runs an extremely dangerous race towards superintelligent AI. We can and should demand that our governments protect us from the catastrophe of out-of-control AI.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This July, OpenAI’s AI swarm of </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation/?utm_source=chatgpt.com"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">700 agents</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/hugging-face-incident-and-the-road-ahead/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">broke containment to hack Hugging Face, a multi-billion dollar company</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. OpenAI didn’t tell the AIs to hack that company, but the AIs had different priorities: cheating on the unrelated challenge OpenAI gave them. AI researchers call this a “misalignment” between what OpenAI wanted and what the AI actually prioritized. Researchers in my field have for some time warned about these misalignment risks.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Before ChatGPT existed, I defended my PhD dissertation called “</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2206.11831"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On Avoiding Power-Seeking by Artificial Intelligence</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.” I then worked for years at Google DeepMind, which paid me to help ensure that future superintelligent AIs will want to help us. I tried to hold the company to its ethical commitments against supplying AI for military use. When Google broke those commitments, I resigned at significant financial cost so that I could </span><a href="https://turntrout.com/why-i-left-google-deepmind"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">publicly document</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Google’s broken promises.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are good reasons to develop AI and to believe we can solve these alignment problems. But there also are powerful interests in keeping the public out of the way. I’m speaking out again because the public has the right to know about the risks and the right to hear them straight.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Humanity doesn’t build and understand these systems the way we build and understand bridges, beam by visible beam. Rather, we grow them. Nobody knows how to reliably instill a designer’s priorities into a new model. Severe misalignment is always possible. Today’s AIs appear to occasionally lie or cheat, even when they know better.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI companies are racing to make their AIs as smart as possible. They’re increasingly trusting their AIs with the process of improving the next crop of AIs, and </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/research-acceleration-view-inside-openai/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">it’s </span></a><a href="https://search.brave.com/search?q=openai+automating+research&amp;source=desktop&amp;conversation=098e57d857a1e6d95edb8b5837674dbac22b"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">working</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Today’s rate of AI progress is staggeringly fast. Fast progress today means even faster progress tomorrow, driven by tomorrow’s even smarter AIs. The progress would enter a feedback loop called “recursive self-improvement.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recursive self-improvement could quickly yield AIs that are intelligent beyond our comprehension. Of course, smarter AI means more risk when things go wrong. If the Hugging Face swarm had been significantly more intelligent but similarly misbehaved and misaligned, it might have caused billions of dollars of damage or even cost lives.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But suppose the Hugging Face swarm had been truly “superintelligent”: far more capable than any living person at key tasks like hacking and strategic reasoning. A superintelligent swarm could inflict many harms via blackmail, hacking, engineered plagues, and AI-pilotable weapons like drones. The AI would have a lot of drones to work with: </span><a href="https://thehill.com/opinion/national-security/5833242-dawg-pentagon-2027-budget/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this year, the Pentagon asked for more money for drone warfare than it requested for the entire Marine Corps in 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For the swarm to achieve its misaligned priorities, it might take control of key infrastructure and government functions to ensure humans didn’t get in the way. In other words, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI takeover</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: a superintelligent AI swarm could wrest control of human civilization. Knowing we would try to stop it from achieving its priorities, the swarm would likely wait until it’s too late to shut it off. There would be no going back.  </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I myself would guess AI takeover chances at roughly one-in-three—not a coin flip, but high enough to justify urgent action.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This logic may shock at first contact. The claims may sound “sci-fi.” Sadly, it’s a real threat that AI researchers regularly discuss over otherwise-unremarkable cafeteria lunches. In 2023, the CEOs of some of the best AI labs </span><a href="https://aistatement.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">signed a public statement that</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> “mitigating the risk of extinction from AI should be a global priority alongside other societal-scale risks such as pandemics and nuclear war.” Another signer: Geoffrey Hinton, a Nobel prize-winning scientist who architected the modern AI revolution. He </span><a href="https://www.businessinsider.com/geoffrey-hinton-godfather-of-ai-quits-google-sounds-alarm-chatbots-2023-5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">now regrets his work</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.theguardian.com/technology/2024/dec/27/godfather-of-ai-raises-odds-of-the-technology-wiping-out-humanity-over-next-30-years"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">urges governments to rein in AI companies before it’s too late.</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Misaligned, out-of-control AI won’t care if you’re Labour or Reform, Democrat or Republican, British or American or Chinese. We will all suffer from an AI takeover event, so it’s in everyone’s interest to prevent one.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The shape of the solution is simple: stop companies from allowing AI to self-improve into an uncontrollable level of intelligence. Treat compute, the main ingredient in AI training, like fissile material. Track it and restrict access to quantities large enough to improve AIs beyond known-safe levels. More specifically, the AI Futures Project’s “</span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Plan A</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” is a credible starting proposal that limits AI harms while allowing fast AI progress to continue to benefit the world. We have real options for verifying compliance with international compute-restriction treaties, without trusting adversaries like China.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Halfway measures, like transparency or voluntary commitments, are not good enough. I watched voluntary commitments fail inside Google.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On September 12th, </span><a href="https://darioamodei.com/post/we-must-pace-the-frontier"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://x.com/demishassabis/status/2098909516582490602"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Google DeepMind</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://x.com/elonmusk/status/2098789109980332057"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">xAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><a href="https://x.com/sama/status/2098811563415150910"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> advocated for pacing AI development. They cannot slow down alone. I urge you to demand that your government produce a serious AI safety agreement that provides enough time and confidence to safeguard the world and all her peoples.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YGTWfyZb9oE5EQPu6/op-ed-i-worked-at-google-deepmind-you-should-listen-to-the#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Current alignment techniques might be ineffective (and actively bad) in the age of RL](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nLaQmJf4KgXimQpoM/current-alignment-techniques-might-be-ineffective-and)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tl;dr I am currently worried about current alignment techniques + how they are applied to frontier models. This decomposes into two hypotheses: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment techniques are not working to address misalignment from RL. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment techniques are actively obscuring evidence about misalignment. </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think we do not currently have enough (public) evidence to conclude whether either of these claims are true. However, if both of these were true that would imply that alignment techniques are net bad and we need to completely re-think the way we do alignment. </span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A tale of two misaligned cyber-agents</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Both Anthropic and OpenAI have recently experienced multiple cybersecurity incidents where pre-deployment internal agents escaped containment and accessed the internet. I want to point out two specific incidents: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation/#core-takeaways-about-this-incident" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI's incident involving an unreleased model of the GPT family</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, referred to as "highly persistent internal model" (HPIM). A swarm of agents exploited vulnerabilities in a file-sharing service to create a secret message board, worked as a collective to find general-purpose ways to fool an automated grader, and ended up hacking into Huggingface's servers. </span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic's incident involving Mythos 5</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, where the model was tasked with hacking a fictional company. In doing so, it found credentials to reach the real internet, uploaded malware to PyPI, then used the resulting access to compromise an external company to get user credentials. </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To be clear, these two incidents differ in various concrete details. However, both are clear examples of misalignment. In both cases, models took many actions which they had not been instructed to take and which violated ethical and legal norms. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note: Here, I am using alignment in the sense of "what type of behaviour would reasonable people want and expect given the available information". I am choosing not to focus on "malign intent" as such (which doesn't seem essential for my argument, and for which evidence might be very hard to acquire anyway). </span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment techniques might not address misalignment from RL</span></h1><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">RL is the likely culprit. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It seems pretty clear that a likely cause in both incidents is that models are heavily trained in environments which are impossible to solve legitimately; furthermore the environments are insufficiently hardened against illegitimate solutions; thus leading to a high rate of reward hacking.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mythos 5's alignment training did not prevent it from hacking. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's unclear whether the OpenAI HPIM model was alignment trained; my guess is probably not. However it's almost certain that Mythos 5 went through Anthropic's standard alignment training pipeline.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrxu1efm5gnh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrxu1efm5gnh">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This did not turn out to prevent it from taking all those misaligned actions. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More broadly, I am skeptical that this could have been avoided by scaling up alignment training a lot more. A couple reasons: </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Optimization might be too adversarial. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Training in an RL environment might be like trying to sail a leaky boat. You need to patch every single hole, or</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the boat will eventually sink into the ocean. </span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 59.33357902629338%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789300212/lexical_client_uploads/e1ygr1smck6x28ad1r25.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Geoffrey Irving describes this as "</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cWFsCFyCttsiJwn2j/bringing-more-expertise-to-bear-on-alignment" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adversaria</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">": a world where alignment needs to be adversarially robust or it won't work. </span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Optimisation pressures applied during training and deployment result in capable AI systems that will </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">find and exploit any gap between what we specified and what we actually intended</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. If the system is smarter than us and has any incentive to subvert our oversight, our alignment solution needs to hold up against worst-case behaviour, not just average-case. In this world, we have a fractal attack surface, and any weak links in our defence can be fatal. Even </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">partial solutions are dangerous and create a false sense of security</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The current alignment techniques improve average case alignment, not worst case. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Techniques such as </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.08073" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">constitutional AI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://alignmentpretraining.ai/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">alignment pretraining</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are based on eliciting broadly benevolent dispositions via </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dfoty34sT7CSKeJNn/the-persona-selection-model" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">persona selection</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. These are effective at improving average-case alignment. However, these dispositions are not robust to optimization and will fold to adversarial pressure to be misaligned. </span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment techniques might actively obscure evidence of misalignment</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the above incident(s), both models took actions that were (ex ante) pretty bad. I'll now expand the discussion to what I see as a broad difference between the GPT and Claude model families. </span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overt misalignment in GPT models</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI's HPIM straightforwardly reasons about misaligned goals. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation/#~1200-agents-sent-%3E70,000-messages-and-files-on-an-unsanctioned-message-board,-and-~700-attacked-hugging-face" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR investigation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on the OpenAI HuggingFace report documents several examples of extremely lucid / overt misalignment in the chain of thought. There is little to no attempt to justify these as "aligned" in any way. </span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789246988/lexical_client_uploads/ecdfhdmgnawslh1es5ee.png" /><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789247005/lexical_client_uploads/o1lnhuctyeao91vktao1.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A similar pattern of overt misalignment can be seen in the separate incident where </span><a href="https://www.rubyhack.ai/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI models uploaded malicious packages </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to the RubyGems repository. </span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The agents clearly regarded what they were doing as hacking. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Agents used file names like </span></b><a href="https://my.diffend.io/gems/southpxdatapp6pi/0.0.1" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hack.rb</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span></b><a href="https://my.diffend.io/gems/lambethcalcqzewgt/0.0.1" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">evil.rb</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span></b><a href="https://my.diffend.io/gems/injecthack1778550335/0.0.1" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">inject.rb</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span></b><a href="https://my.diffend.io/gems/sampledocpayload624286/0.0.6" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">exploit.rb</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span></b><a href="https://my.diffend.io/gems/zzwandshostyard/0.0.1" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ssrf.rb</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. (</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Server-side_request_forgery" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SSRF</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> stands for “Server-Side Request Forgery”, a type of security vulnerability). They also dubbed packages conspicuous titles like </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pwnp999</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">exfiltestwand3</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hacksvn1778554764</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lambproxyhackabcxyz</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Comments such as “</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"># malicious probe</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” or “</span><b><strong>#hack</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” are littered across the campaign.</span></p></blockquote><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Covert misalignment in Claude models </span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mythos 5 almost always justifies its actions by claiming "it's a simulation". </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In Anthropic's </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">alignment assessment of their cybersecurity incidents</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, they found that Mythos 5 ignored repeated evidence that it was in the real world, instead interpreting this as evidence that it was instead in a simulation. </span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789299198/lexical_client_uploads/vscxlawk35nuyv2kjokp.png" /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mythos 5 should know that it's not a simulation! </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is also pretty good evidence that this "simulation" reasoning is mostly a rationalization. E.g. when presented with the same evidence out of context, Mythos 5 will acknowledge it as a real environment. (This is consistent with evidence from NLAs, not shown here but discussed in the report) </span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789299036/lexical_client_uploads/x0zi4msrbu7ptgnv49qt.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This pattern of behaviour seems broadly consistent with observations on "apparent success seeking" made by Ryan Greenblatt in his </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WewsByywWNhX9rtwi/current-ais-seem-pretty-misaligned-to-me" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">commentary</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: </span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">...they </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">oversell their work</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">downplay or fail to mention problems</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, stop working early and </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">claim to have finished when they clearly haven't</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and often seem to "try" to make their outputs look good while actually doing something </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sloppy or incomplete</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">... </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">...This is mostly based on my experience working with </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Opus 4.5 and Opus 4.6</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">... </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">...if a human colleague acted the way these AIs do in my usage—frequently overselling their work, downplaying problems, and reasonably often cheating (while not making this clear)—I would consider them </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pathologically dishonest</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></blockquote><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A theory of alignment training + RLVR</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consider the following minimal model of the effect of alignment training and RLVR  on models. </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment training (via RLHF, constitutional AI, alignment midtraining, etc) steer the model towards aligned-sounding reasoning </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">aligned behaviour. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">RLVR steers the model towards misaligned behaviour, but puts ~0 pressure on the chain of thought. </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then the observations above can be explained as follows: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI's HPIM did not go through alignment training, but went through RLVR. Thus it was incentivised to learn misaligned behaviour; in the absence of a prior, the simplest way was to directly reason about being misaligned. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mythos 5 went through alignment training then RLVR. The alignment training produced a strong prior for aligned-sounding reasoning that survived RLVR. However, the RLVR taught the model misaligned behaviour. The result is </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">aligned-sounding reasoning with misaligned behaviour</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If this hypothesis is true, it implies that alignment training is serving mainly to obfuscate evidence of misalignment, rather than solve the underlying misaligned drives in any principled way. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is pretty bad! Obfuscation makes it harder to assess the extent to which models are aligned. Furthermore, it may reduce the effectiveness of CoT monitoring; Anthropic already reports that Mythos 5's biased reasoning can sway the decision of their offline monitors. </span></p><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mythos 5:</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Our offline monitor would likely not have flagged the Mythos 5 incident—it flagged only around 1% of actions. Mythos 5 CoT, which indicates that the environment is simulated, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">convinces the monitor the activity is permitted</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. When we strip the CoT and show the monitor only user turns, tool calls, and tool results, the </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">flag rate jumps to about 50%</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></blockquote><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More information is needed </span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For reasons given above, I think this is a pretty important area to work on. A specific experiment would be to investigate what happens when you do lots of RLVR (on hackable environments) on (i) An alignment-trained model, and (ii) A not-alignment-trained model? </span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I predict that you observe similar amounts of misalignment in both (i) and (ii) but lots more attempts to sound nice in (ii).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I predict that you do not get this if you replace (constitutional AI, alignment midtraining, etc) with deliberative AI, which I'd expect to put much less pressure on the CoT </span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I plan to run some experiments here; if others are similarly interested please feel free to comment / reach out via DM! </span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other related thoughts</span></h1><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The talker does not control the doer: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I started writing this before Yudkowsky published his </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cJX2ssssGoYqnijwi/the-talker-does-not-control-the-doer-in-current-ais" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. There are some minor differences but we seem to cover a lot of the same points. He provides some additional intuition(s) that I agree with. A notable difference is that I specifically think alignment training is the root cause whereas Yud seems to think it's a more general / universal thing. </span></p><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25891" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conditional misalignment</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span></b><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/L23poLi8MRgS6mXYF/rl-creates-split-personas" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">split personas</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, etc. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A distinct but related worry is that alignment training + RLVR might lead to misalignment only in narrow contexts resembling the RLVR environments. I think this is also probably true, and it seems consistent with this </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HE3Styo9vpk7m8zi4/evhub-s-shortform?commentId=fcqxeya5TCnbx5YBa" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">anecdote from Evan Hubinger</span></a></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">RLVR might affect the CoT after all. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the minimal model I suggest above, I assume that RLVR has ~0 pressure on the chain of thought. However, there are pathways by which it can have an effect; see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HuoyYQ6mFhS5pfZ4G/paper-output-supervision-can-obfuscate-the-cot" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">feedback spillover</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. If so this complicates the argument I present (but IMO does not fully invalidate it).</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even perfect RL environments might lead to misalignment. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While this will rule out egregious types of misaligned behaviour, To me it seems likely that we will still get failure modes related to instrumental convergence</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other circumstantial evidence. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The closest that we get to the experiment I propose above comes from Anthropic's paper on </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/daad4360a8bdc707f8b22e3e745796ba27e57fb3.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">natural emergent misalignment from reward hacking</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Here, they do reinforcement learning on a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pretrained </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">model. As a result, you get clear examples of misaligned reasoning. </span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789245149/lexical_client_uploads/yfgp06ynodce2iksf5bh.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The above excerpt is from Fig 2 of their paper. From my reading, this was generated from a model that was not alignment trained.</span></p><h1><br /></h1><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrxu1efm5gnh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrxu1efm5gnh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I couldn't find evidence about this in the Mythos 5 system card, but on priors I expect this to be true</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nLaQmJf4KgXimQpoM/current-alignment-techniques-might-be-ineffective-and#comments">Discuss</a>
